Let me plan the proof. We need strong normalization for STLC CBV with locally nameless syntax.

The main challenge: the typing rule T_Abs uses `L : list atom` for cofinite quantification, so we get the body opened with a fresh free variable.

Standard approach: logical relation indexed by type:
- `SN t := strongly_normalizing t`
- `LR Bool t := SN t` (or value t /\ SN t? For SN we usually just need SN t and that's closed under expansion)
- `LR (T1 -> T2) t := SN t /\ forall v, value v -> LR T1 v -> LR T2 (tm_app t v)`. Or for CBV: `LR (T1->T2) t := SN t /\ forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u)`.

Actually for strong normalization of CBV, the classic Girard-Tait style works. Let's define:

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => strongly_normalizing t /\ forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u)
  end.
```

Hmm, but for CBV with the ST_App2 rule, applying a non-value thing... Actually with the LR we need to show:
- If LR T t then SN t (built into the arrow case, but for Bool it's `SN t`, need for arrow to be sound).
- LR is closed under "head expansion": if t --> t' and LR T t' then LR T t? Actually we need backward closure (anti-reduction): if t --> t' and LR T t' then LR T t. This holds since SN is backward closed. For arrow: LR T1->T2 t requires SN t and forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (t u). If t --> t', then t u --> t' u (ST_App1 requires locally_closed u — but LR T1 u doesn't necessarily imply locally_closed u!). Hmm. That's an issue: ST_App1 has side condition `locally_closed t2`. Since we're in locally nameless with fvars, and typing gives... hmm, does typing imply locally closed at 0? Not necessarily: free variables allowed. `lc_at 0` fails for any free bvar, but fvars are fine. So a well-typed term under empty context, is it locally closed? T_Var requires Gamma x = Some T, and with empty that's impossible. So closed terms have no fvars? Actually T_Var with empty context requires `empty x = Some T`, i.e., `None = Some T`, contradiction. So well-typed closed terms have no free fvars either but they may still have bvars? Let's see: T_Abs introduces body as `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` which has bvars replaced by fvar x, and recursively opens. The initial `t` might have dangling bvars though — T_Abs's hypothesis is only about opened body. Hmm, for a bare `tm_bvar 0` under empty context there's no typing rule. The typing relation is on the given syntax. So a term like `tm_abs T (tm_bvar 5)` — T_Abs hypothesis: for fresh x, `open (tm_bvar 5) (fvar x)` = `tm_bvar 5` since 5 ≠ 0... Well, need <{ x |-> T1 ; Gamma |-- tm_bvar 5 \in T2 }> which is not derivable. So it won't type. Still, formally, does has_type empty t T imply locally_closed t? Let's think: it should, by induction on typing derivation. For T_Abs: need lc_at 0 (tm_abs T t1), i.e. lc_at 1 t1. Hmm — we know lc_at 0 (open t1 (fvar x)) for fresh x. Does that imply lc_at 1 t1? Actually `open t1 (fvar x)` replaces bvar 0 with fvar x in t1 but leaves higher bvars. lc_at 0 (open t1 (fvar x)) means no bvar at all ≥ 0 in the opened term; then t1 has no bvar ≥ 1, so lc_at 1 t1. Wait, is that right: open_rec 0 replaces bvar 0, but bvars ≥ 1 remain. So lc_at 0 of opened means no remaining bvars, i.e., all bvars in t1 are 0 (all occurrences? open_rec replaces all occurrences of bvar 0 at depth 0, and inside abs it becomes depth 1 so bvar 0 remains). Hmm, tm_abs T (tm_bvar 0) opened at 0: open_rec 0 u (tm_abs T (tm_bvar 0)) = tm_abs T (open_rec 1 u (tm_bvar 0)) = tm_abs T (tm_bvar 0). So opened body still has bvar 0 under the binder. Then lc_at 0 of that term fails!? `lc_at 0 (tm_abs T (tm_bvar 0))` requires `lc_at 1 (tm_bvar 0)` which holds since 0 < 1. Good. So lc_at 0 (tm_abs T t1) = lc_at 1 t1, and t1 = tm_bvar 0 is fine.

Hmm so is lc_at 0 (open t1 (fvar x)) ⟹ lc_at 1 t1? Consider t1 = tm_bvar 0: open gives tm_bvar 0 (at depth 0) → fvar x, lc_at 0 holds; t1 = bvar 0 has lc_at 1. Fine. Consider t1 = tm_abs T' (tm_bvar 1): open_rec 0 gives tm_abs T' (open_rec 1 ... (tm_bvar 1)) = tm_abs T' (tm_bvar 1). lc_at 0 of that requires lc_at 1 (tm_bvar 1), which fails. Good.

So well-typed closed terms are locally closed (fvar-free and all bvars bound). Anyway.

But do we need locally_closed in the LR? The step relation's ST_App1 requires `locally_closed t2` and ST_App2 requires `value v1`, and ST_AppAbs requires value v and lc of abs.

For the fundamental theorem, we need the LR to handle application: for t1 : T1 -> T2 and t2 : T1, show SN (t1 t2). With the arrow LR giving `forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (t1 u)`, we'd apply with u = t2. Then need LR T1 t2 from the fundamental theorem on t2. Great — so the fundamental theorem gives LR for closed terms directly (not SN only). That works nicely without needing locally_closed of t2 in the reduction analysis: to show SN (t1 t2), we need to consider all steps t1 t2 --> t'. Cases:
- ST_AppAbs: t1 = tm_abs T1' t, value v = t2, t' = open t t2. This requires t2 to be a value and the abs locally closed. Then how do we get SN of open t t2? From typing... hmm. This is the key case (the "beta" case). We need: if the abstraction `tm_abs T1 t` is well-typed under Gamma, and v is in LR T1, and it's a value, then LR T2 (open t v).
- ST_App1: t1 --> t1', needs lc(t2); t' = t1' t2. Then we'd use the induction that t1 is SN: SN(t1) gives SN(t1')? Actually SN_intro says: t is SN if all its one-step reducts are SN. So we need SN(t1' t2). Hmm, but the arrow LR for t1 gives `forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (t1 u)` for the original t1, not for t1'. Hmm. Standard technique: define LR arrow as `SN t /\ forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (t u)`, then to prove SN(t1 t2), use ST_App1 case: t1 --> t1' with lc(t2); need SN(t1' t2). We don't know LR arrow of t1' unless we have anti-reduction closure for LR at arrow type. Anti-reduction: if t1 --> t1' and LR T t1' then LR T t1. Then from t1 SN... hmm.

Alternative: Use the classic "SN of application" lemma proven by induction on SN derivations. Let me think about the standard structure.

Actually the cleanest: Define LR as:
- LR Bool t := SN t
- LR (T1→T2) t := SN t ∧ ∀u, LR T1 u → LR T2 (t·u)

Lemma LR_SN: LR T t → SN t (by induction on T; for arrow it's a component, for Bool it's identity).

Lemma LR_anti: LR T t' → t --> t' → LR T t? Hmm, for the arrow case, this requires for all u in LR T1: LR T2 (t u) given LR T2 (t' u) and t u --> t' u (ST_App1 with lc u). So we need LR T1 u → locally_closed u. Do we have that? LR T1 u implies SN u; does SN imply locally_closed? No, as terms can contain fvars (fvar x is SN trivially since no steps). Hmm, but ST_App1 requires locally_closed t2. Hmm, this is an annoyance of the language definition: applications only step when the argument is locally closed.

LR Bool t := SN t  (maybe ∧ locally_closed t?)

Actually, let's think about where lc(u) is needed:
1. ST_App1's premise `locally_closed t2` — this is on the *argument* t2 in the app. So when reasoning about steps of (t1 t2), the ST_App1 case gives us lc(t2) directly as a hypothesis! Great: in the SN(t1 t2) proof, the ST_App1 case has lc(t2) as hypothesis. So we don't need LR T1 u → lc u there.

- R_Bool = { t | t is a closed value of type Bool } ... no, TAPL 12.2 uses a different style for weak normalization.

R_Bool = {t | t is strongly normalizing}

Let me look at this from the perspective of the "Kripke-style" or "reducibility candidates" approach: We can define a stronger notion: 

Define `LR T t` simultaneously with `SN t`, and prove two facts by mutual induction on type:
- LR T t → SN t
- LR T t is closed backward under steps (anti-reduction).
- (head expansion) if t' ∈ LR T and t --> t' then t ∈ LR T.

Hmm, let me look at this from a different angle. The classic proof uses the fact that LR is defined on values and the "semantic" application, then the fundamental theorem uses a "backward closure" lemma that includes the beta case.

I recall the actual structure: TAPL 12.2 uses "if t --> t' and t' ∈ R_T then t ∈ R_T" with conditions? Let me recall the proof of the fundamental theorem in TAPL for SN:

Theorem 12.2.5 (?): If Gamma |- t : T and all variables in Gamma are in R, then t ∈ R_T.
Proof by induction on typing derivation. The abstraction case: t = λx:T1.t2, need λx:T1.t2 ∈ R_{T1→T2}: 
 - it's SN (values are SN)

   Proof: by induction on SN(s)? Since s ∈ R_{T1} → s ∈ SN by 12.2.2. Hmm, they do an induction on the structure: "since s ∈ SN, induction on that derivation"? Actually I recall: "By induction on the derivation of s ∈ SN" — the application (λx.t2) s reduces either by ST_App2 (s --> s') to (λx.t2) s' and we use IH, or by ST_AppAbs to [x↦s]t2 which is in R_{T2} by the IH of the typing derivation (since s ∈ R_{T1})... but wait the abstraction body typing IH gives: for all s ∈ R_{T1}, [x↦s]t2 ∈ R_{T2}. Yes! That's the fundamental theorem's IH applied to the body with the extended context.

So the beta case works via the typing IH directly. And the ST_App2 case works via induction on SN(s). Good.

And the application case: t1 t2 : T2 where t1 : T1→T2 and t2 : T1. IH gives t1 ∈ R_{T1→T2} and t2 ∈ R_{T1}. By definition of arrow, t1 t2 ∈ R_{T2}. Done directly! No need for extra lemma. 

So the fundamental theorem is proven by induction on typing derivation; the only tricky part is the abstraction case, where we need SN of the abstraction (easy) plus closure of application under SN-induction on the argument.

In the abstraction case for CBV: (λx:T1.t2) s where s ∈ R_{T1}:
- s ∈ SN (from R ⊆ SN).
- Prove by induction on SN(s) derivation: (λx.t2) s ∈ R_{T2}.
  - Steps from (λx.t2) s: 
    - ST_App2 with s --> s': need (λx.t2) s' ∈ R_{T2}, which follows from IH on SN s' (since SN_intro gives for all s' with s --> s', SN s', and IH).
    - ST_AppAbs: if lc(λx.t2) and value s: result is open t2 s, which is in R_{T2} by typing IH applied to s (need s ∈ R_{T1}, which we have). But careful: typing gives `open t2 (fvar x) : T2` under x:T1, Gamma, and the IH says for all s ∈ R_{T1}, the substitution instance is in R_{T2}. We need a substitution lemma connecting `open t2 (fvar x)` with substitution of s.
    
So we need a substitution lemma: If Gamma, x:T1 |- open t (fvar x) : T2 and s ∈ R_{T1}, then [x ↦ s](open t (fvar x))... Actually we need to phrase the fundamental theorem with a context interpretation: Gamma ⊨ rho. Since we're using named fvars, the standard locally nameless approach:

Define environment/context interpretation: `Gamma ⊨ rho` iff for all x T, Gamma x = Some T → R_T (rho x). Then prove:

Fundamental theorem: if Gamma |- t : T and Gamma ⊨ rho, then R_T (subst rho t), where subst replaces fvars by terms.

But T_Abs's hypothesis is `open t1 (fvar x)` for fresh x, and the substitution instance would be `subst rho (open t1 (fvar x))`. Then when applying to s, we need `subst (rho[x↦s]) t1` vs `[x↦s](subst rho (open t1 (fvar x)))`. There's a classic "open/close" lemma: if x not in domain of rho... Since we choose x fresh (not in L and not in free vars of the terms involved), and rho only maps variables... Hmm. We can define notation:

`subst rho t := fmap over fvars`. Then `open t (fvar x)` = `subst (x ↦ fvar x) t`? Actually opening with fvar x replaces bvar 0 with fvar x. And substituting all free variables with rho. The composition: `subst rho (open t1 (fvar x))` = `subst (update_env rho x (fvar x)) t1` for x fresh w.r.t. rho? Since subst replaces fvar x by rho x, and if x is the fresh variable (not in the domain of rho i.e. rho maps only things in Gamma; but rho could be arbitrary). Standard technique: prove a lemma about `subst rho (open t (fvar x))` where x notin free vars of rho's image... Hmm.

Alternative much simpler approach for this benchmark: Since the context is empty in the final theorem, maybe we can avoid full context-substitution machinery. The typing rule T_Abs introduces a variable x with context x |-> T1 ; Gamma. We could do the fundamental theorem with a "semantic context" but a simpler alternative: use the standard locally-nameless route where we prove:

`If Gamma |- t : T then for all rho with Gamma ⊨ rho, R_T (subst rho t)`.

Hmm, but T_Abs's body is `open t1 (fvar x)` with x fresh. We need to show `R_{T1→T2} (subst rho (tm_abs T1 t1))`. Note subst rho (tm_abs T1 t1) = tm_abs T1 (subst rho t1) if subst doesn't go under binders (which it doesn't - fvars only). Then for s ∈ R_{T1}, we need `R_{T2} ((tm_abs T1 (subst rho t1)) · s)`. Beta step gives `open (subst rho t1) s`. Hmm, the typing IH applies to `open t1 (fvar x)` substituted: `subst (rho[x↦s]) (open t1 (fvar x))`. Need equality with `open (subst rho t1) s` (assuming x fresh enough).

Let's just prove a general lemma:
`subst (update_env rho x s) (open t (fvar x)) = open (subst rho t) s` provided x is not in the free variables of `subst rho t`... Actually since substitution into the body: `open t (fvar x)` replaces bvar 0 with fvar x. Then subst with rho[x↦s] replaces fvar x with s. On the other side, subst rho t then open with s replaces bvar 0 with s. These are equal provided x is not free in any rho y for y in... The issue: bvar 0 replaced by fvar x then by s vs. directly by s: fine. Other fvars y ≠ x: replaced by rho y on both sides fine. But if x occurs... x could be a fvar inside t? If x occurs free in t, then `open t (fvar x)` keeps it (open only replaces bvars), and subst rho[x↦s] replaces it with s, while subst rho t gives rho x and open doesn't change it. So need x ∉ FV(t). And bvar indices under binders: subst doesn't go under binders, no capture issues since substitution is for fvars. Since x is chosen fresh from L (a list of atoms), we need to ensure x ∉ FV(t1) — we can always pick such x if we have infinitely many atoms and L is finite, but FV(t1) is finite too. Hmm, but the T_Abs rule gives us the hypothesis for ALL x ∉ L. We can choose any x we want not in L ∪ FV(stuff). So we can arrange.

Actually, hold on. Maybe we can shortcut: since the target is only for the empty context, we could set up the fundamental theorem with environment rho, and handle the abstraction case by choosing x fresh w.r.t. a finite set including FV of t1 and the domain of rho... but rho's domain is infinite (a function atom → tm). Hmm, but we can define rho with finite domain by design: `Gamma ⊨ rho` only constrains rho on variables in Gamma's finite domain. Actually a cleaner approach: define rho as an association list or a function with finite domain (e.g., `env := list (atom * tm)`). Then "x fresh" means x not in dom(L) and dom(rho) and FV(t1), all finite. 

Alternatively, use the classic approach: avoid `open`/`subst` composition issues by defining the semantic context via a function `rho : atom -> tm` and using a substitution that's defined on fvars, and prove the lemma

`Lemma subst_open: forall rho x s t, x \notin FV(t) -> subst (update rho x s) (open t (fvar x)) = open (subst rho t) s.`

where FV is defined as a predicate/ list. Since we need freshness w.r.t. FV(t1), and x is chosen fresh w.r.t. L, we need FV(t1) finite... Alternatively choose x fresh w.r.t. L ∪ dom considerations. Hmm, T_Abs gives "forall x, ~ In x L -> ...". We can instantiate at a specific x as long as we can prove ~In x L. If we define L' = L ++ (list of all fvars occurring in t1) ++ (dom of rho) and we can't control L. But we can choose x = some atom not in L' — need "exists x, x ∉ L'". We can prove by picking a fresh atom: e.g., `fresh (L : list atom) := 1 + max of list` or use `Nat.max` fold. Simple.

But actually, is the freshness w.r.t. FV(t1) even needed? Let's re-examine: We want to prove R_{T2} of `open (subst rho t1) s` given the IH: for the fresh x, `x |-> T1 ; Gamma |- open t1 (fvar x) : T2` and the environment interpretation for `x |-> T1 ; Gamma` at rho' = rho[x↦s]... but note the context is `update Gamma x T1`, and the env is rho[x↦s]. The IH yields `R_{T2} (subst rho' (open t1 (fvar x)))`.

Now, `subst rho' (open t1 (fvar x))` vs `open (subst rho t1) s`. If x ∈ FV(t1): LHS substitutes that occurrence with s; RHS keeps rho x. So we need x ∉ FV(t1) — unless we arrange rho x = ... no, rho is fixed by the environment interpretation (only constrained on Gamma's domain though). Hmm. Since x ∉ dom(Gamma) (x fresh from L which we can take to include dom Gamma... actually T_Abs's L is given, we can't add to it, but we can choose x ∉ L ∪ FV(t1) ∪ dom(Gamma)), but rho's value at x is unconstrained if x ∉ dom(Gamma) — the env interpretation only requires rho x ∈ R_T when Gamma x = Some T. So we could set rho differently... but rho is fixed.

OK here's the standard resolution: define the context interpretation using finite maps (list-based env) so that "rho" has finite domain and we choose x fresh w.r.t. it. That's clean.

Alternatively — and this is common in locally nameless formalizations — define substitution on fvars with `subst` and use explicitly the lemma:

`Lemma open_subst: forall (rho : atom -> tm) x s t, x ∉ FV t -> x ∉ FV of images...`

Hmm, the images issue: subst rho t replaces fvar y with rho y; if x occurs free in some rho y for y ∈ FV(t), then LHS's occurrence of fvar x (from rho y) gets replaced by s, whereas RHS's doesn't. So we need x ∉ FV(rho y) for all y, OR restrict rho's image to not contain x. With a finite-domain env we can ensure... no, the images can contain fvars.

Ugh. The classic way to avoid this: use a different notion of substitution where the environment is applied only to the term's free variables, and choose x fresh w.r.t. all fvars in the environment's image too.   | ... 
  end
```

Let me think about the actual mathematics. In CBV STLC, strong normalization is true. The standard proof (e.g., in Girard's "Proofs and Types" for call-by-name, or the CBV version) uses a "reducibility" definition where for arrow types you require closure under application with reducible arguments, and the key lemma is:

"If t ∈ R_{T1→T2} and s ∈ R_{T1}, then t s ∈ R_{T2}".

- If s is a value and lc: beta → [s/x]t ∈ R_{T2} (by IH of typing/ fundamental theorem on t).

Lemma `app_sn`: If SN t1, SN t2, and t1's "body semantics"... Let me think about the actual structure used in e.g. the "Stlc" chapter of Software Foundations' "Strong normalization" (there's an exercise). Or the locally nameless tutorial by Charguéraud ("Locally Nameless" tutorial) — it ends with the STLC SN proof! Yes: Charguéraud's locally nameless tutorial (the "Locally Nameless" lecture notes) proves strong normalization for STLC with a logical relation, using `body` lemmas, `open`/`close`, etc.

I recall the approach in "Tutorial on the locally nameless representation" by Charguéraud, Section on STLC: defines `trm`, `open`, `close`, `body`, and proves type soundness (progress/preservation), not SN. Hmm.

Alternatively, the POPLmark / "strong normalization" formalizations in Coq: e.g. the file `SN.v` in some developments. The typical structure:

```
Definition SN (t : tm) := Acc step t.
Definition RED (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Bool => SN
  | A → B => fun t => SN t /\ forall u, RED A u -> RED B (App t u)
  end.
```

Key lemmas:
1. RED_SN: RED T t → SN t.
2. RED_anti / "head expansion": If t --> t' and RED T t', then RED T t. — This requires for arrow: given RED B (t' u), show RED B (t u) — needs t u --> t' u which needs lc(u). Hmm.

Alternatively:
3. SN_App: if SN t1 and (∀ t1', t1 -->* t1' ...) hmm.

Honestly, maybe the cleanest route around the `locally_closed t2` side-condition is to prove `LR T u → locally_closed u` by making lc part of the LR. But we saw backward lc preservation issues... wait, do we need backward lc preservation? Let's re-examine. Where do we need lc?

Uses of ST_App1/ST_App2 require lc(t2)/value(v1) premises which are *given* when we invert a step. So we only need lc facts when we want to *construct* a step. Where do we construct steps?
(a) In forward closure: t --> t' → R_T t → R_T t'. For arrow: need R_{T2}(t' u) from R_{T2}(t u); constructing t u --> t' u needs lc(u). So we need lc(u) for u ∈ R_{T1}. If lc is part of the LR, we have it.
(b) In proving SN of applications in the T_Abs case... we didn't need to construct steps there except via the SN_intro inversion.

So if LR includes lc, we can do forward closure. Let's check forward closure with lc included:
- T = Bool: R_Bool = SN ∧ lc. t --> t', SN t → SN t' easy (SN_intro inversion). lc t' from lc t and step: need "lc preserved forward by step" lemma.
- T = T1→T2: need SN t', lc t', and ∀u ∈ R_{T1}, R_{T2}(t' u).
  - SN t' ✓ from SN t.
  - lc t' from lc t and step ✓ (lemma).
  - For u ∈ R_{T1}: R_{T1} gives lc u, so t u --> t' u by ST_App1 (needs t --> t' and lc u). Then R_{T2}(t u) from the arrow component, and IH (forward closure at T2, structural recursion on T2) gives R_{T2}(t' u). ✓ 

Great, forward closure works by induction on T if lc is in the LR and we have forward-lc-preservation for step.

Now what about the T_Abs case proving R_{T1→T2}(λ.body)? Need:
- SN(λ.body): λ.body is a value with no steps → SN_intro with no reducts. Need: no step from an abstraction ✓ (inversion).
- lc(λ.body): need lc_at 1 body. Hmm, from typing? The body `open t1 (fvar x)` is typed under x:T1;Gamma — does typing imply lc? For the locally-nameless typing relation with opening, yes: has_type Gamma t T → lc_at 0 t (for fvars unconstrained, they're lc). Actually for context with fvars, lc_at 0 still holds if all bvars are bound. We can prove `has_type Gamma t T → locally_closed t` by induction — need lc_at 0 of `open t1 (fvar x)` implying lc_at 1 t1, which I showed above holds regardless of fvar (since fvar is lc). Let's verify: from lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1) conclude lc_at 1 t1: For t1 = bvar i: open gives (if i=0 then fvar x else bvar i) which is lc_at 0. Need lc_at 1 (bvar i) i.e. i < 1 i.e. i = 0. If i ≠ 0 then open gives bvar i with lc_at 0 → i < 0, contradiction. So i = 0 ✓. Induction on t1 structure with the "for all k" generalization. Standard lemma: `lc_at k (open_rec k u t) → lc_at (S k) t` (given nothing about u? let's check t = bvar k case: gives lc_at k u; conclusion lc_at (S k) (bvar k) ✓ trivially). For abs: lc_at k (abs T (open_rec (S k) u body)) → lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S k) u body) → IH gives lc_at (S (S k)) body → lc_at (S k) (abs T body) ✓. For app: both parts. For fvar/true/false trivial. So yes: lemma `lc_open_inv : forall k u t, lc_at k (open_rec k u t) -> lc_at (S k) t`. 

  So `has_type Gamma t T → lc_at 0 t` by induction on the typing derivation, using that lemma for the abs case. And for T_Var, fvar is lc ✓.

  Then lc(λ.body): the T_Abs case needs lc_at 1 t1 where the body in the typing hypothesis is `open t1 (fvar x)`. We get lc_at 0 (open t1 (fvar x)) from the IH, then lc_open_inv gives lc_at 1 t1 ✓. Hmm, let me simplify: instead of Gamma being a function atom → option ty, note the actual contexts are built as `x |-> T1 ; Gamma`. The env rho corresponds structurally. Maybe define the env as a function `atom -> tm` and the semantic context interpretation as:

```

But then the freshness issue for `subst rho (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst rho t1) s`: rho is a total function; we need x ∉ FV(rho y) for all y ∈ FV(t1) — infinitely many constraints? We can *modify* rho: define rho' = fun y => if y = x then s else rho y, and the env_sem for the extended context requires rho' to be LR at the right types. The equality we need is:

`subst rho' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst rho t1) s` where rho' = rho[x ↦ s].

This requires x ∉ FV(rho y) for y ∈ FV(t1) (else the occurrence of fvar x inside rho y gets replaced by s on LHS but not RHS). Hmm, with a function rho we can't guarantee that. But we can *choose a different rho*? No, rho is given.

Wait — actually we can use a trick: the env_sem is only about Gamma's domain. Variables not in Gamma's domain can be mapped to anything. Since rho is given with potentially arbitrary images, we can define a *new* environment rho0 = fun y => if y ∈ dom(Gamma) then rho y else tm_fvar y (identity outside). Then env_sem Gamma rho0 holds if env_sem Gamma rho holds, and rho0 is "identity outside a finite set". Then FV(rho0 y) ⊆ {y} for y ∉ dom Gamma, so the set of atoms in images is finite-ish: for y ∈ FV(t1) ∩ dom(Gamma) finitely many, plus y itself. So we can choose x fresh w.r.t. all of those. 

Even simpler: use finite env (list). Then the "atoms in env" is computable. I'll go with a finite list env. Let me define:

```
Definition env := list (atom * tm).

Fixpoint env_lookup (x : atom) (e : env) : option tm :=
  match e with
  | [] => None
  | (y, s) :: e' => if Nat.eqb x y then Some s else env_lookup x e'
  end.

Fixpoint subst (e : env) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar n => tm_bvar n
  | tm_fvar x => match env_lookup x e with Some s => s | None => tm_fvar x end
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst e t1) (subst e t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst e t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.
```

And `env_atoms e := flat_map (fun p => p.1 :: fv_list p.2) e` where fv_list lists the fvars in a term.

And `env_sem Gamma e := forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> exists s, env_lookup x e = Some s /\ LR T s /\ lc s` plus maybe all pairs in e are lc: `forall x s, env_lookup x e = Some s -> lc s`.

Hmm, we also need, for the equality `subst (rho[x↦s]) (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst rho t1) s`, that the *other* variables' images don't contain x. Wait, actually we need `x ∉ dom(rho)`; but also for other y's: lookup y (update e x s) = lookup y e requires y ≠ x ✓.

And also we need `x ∉ FV(rho y)` for y in FV(t1) — captured by x ∉ env_atoms rho.

Hmm, actually there's a subtlety: after substituting with rho, the images rho y for y ∈ FV(t1) appear in `open (subst rho t1) s`. If x is a free variable of some image, then in LHS, substituting rho[x↦s] replaces fvar x occurrences inside rho y with s. So indeed need x ∉ FV(rho y).

So define `atoms_of_env` to include all fvars in all images and all domain atoms.

Then also `x ∉ FV(t1)`.

Now, can we always choose such an x? Yes: pick x = fresh from the finite list L ++ fv_list t1 ++ atoms_of_env rho. Need a "fresh atom" function: e.g., `S (fold_right max 0 L)`. Provable: for all x in L, x < fresh L, so fresh ∉ L.

Hmm wait, but do we even need to be that careful? We know `forall x, x ∉ L -> x |-> T1 ; Gamma |- open t1 (fvar x) : T2`. We choose x fresh from L + extras. ✓.

Now, back to the main structural question: the T_Abs case and the induction on SN(s) for the argument.

Let me restate the plan with LR including lc:

```
Definition LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => strongly_normalizing t /\ (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Hmm, recursive definition over T with LR defined as Fixpoint. Since LR appears in a negative position? `LR (T1→T2) t := lc t ∧ SN t ∧ ∀u, LR T1 u → LR T2 (t u)` — LR T1 and LR T2 are subterms of T, so a Fixpoint on T is fine structurally. But `LR T1 u` appears in a premise (negative position), which for a Fixpoint returning Prop is fine (it's a function, not inductively defined).

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\ 
                      (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Note: `LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u)` — Coq might complain about the recursive call `LR T1 u` in a negative position? For Fixpoints returning Prop, Coq allows recursive occurrences in any position as long as the structural argument decreases? Actually, for Fixpoint definitions, the check is on the structural recursion (T decreases), and Prop-valued fixpoints can have negative occurrences (this is how accessibility/Acc works... Acc is Inductive though). Hmm, `Fixpoint f (T : ty) := match T with ... => (forall u, f T1 u -> f T2 (app t u)) end` — Coq's guard checker only cares about structural recursion, not positivity, for Fixpoint. I believe this is accepted. Let me test quickly. Actually there might be an issue with "not a truly recursive definition"? No, it's structurally recursive on T. Should be fine.

Hmm. Let me look at the actual math. In CBV STLC, the proof of the "abstraction is reducible" case is usually done as:

R_{A→B} = { t | SN t ∧ ∀ v, value v → R_A v → R_B (t v) }  -- quantifying only over values!

Let's step back and think about the mathematical essence. We want a set R_B satisfying:
(1) R_B ⊆ SN.
(2) For abstraction: (∀s ∈ R_A, R_B(open body s)) → ∀s ∈ R_A, R_B((λx.body)s).
(3) Application: t ∈ R_{A→B} ∧ s ∈ R_A → t s ∈ R_B (by def).

For (2), given s ∈ R_A ⊆ SN, we can induct on SN s. Reducts of (λ.body)s that are of the form (λ.body)s' with s --> s' — use IH, which needs R_A s' — forward closure (which we established works with lc in the LR). The beta reduct open body s ∈ R_B — from typing. So R_B((λ.body)s) follows from backward closure at B. **Backward closure at B is the key**: if t --> t' and R_B t' then R_B t. 

Backward closure at B = C→D: need SN t (from SN t' via SN_intro: a step t --> t' means all reducts of t are... wait SN t from SN t' requires showing all reducts of t are SN: for any t --> t'', hmm, we know t --> t'. Doesn't directly give SN t. Standard: SN t iff all one-step reducts SN. Given SN t', we need all reducts t'' of t to be SN. If there's only one reduct chain... In general, t --> t'' and t --> t' — we need SN t''. Hmm, backward closure of SN: if t --> t' and SN t' then SN t? That's not true in general for branching (t may have other reducts t'' not related to t'). But actually, SN is defined as "all reduction paths finite", and if t --> t' with SN t', then SN t requires all reducts SN; but the reduct t'' could be different... However, in our setting, the step relation is deterministic-ish? Not for arbitrary terms... For the beta case, (λ.body)s has beta reduct and ST_App2 reducts; both are handled separately: SN((λ.body)s) proven by SN_intro considering each reduct individually.

So backward closure of R_B should be stated as: to prove R_B t, use SN_intro: for each reduct t' of t, R_B t'. Wait, but for the arrow case, R_B t requires ∀w ∈ R_C, R_D(t w), and we'd prove that by showing every reduct of t w is in R_D and using SN_intro (need SN(t w) too). Hmm, SN(t w) is needed as a component. This is the "SN + all reducts reducible" characterization:

**Characterization:** R_B t iff SN t ∧ ∀ t', t --> t' → R_B t'. Hmm, this is circular but suggests a coinduction-like/SN-recursion definition:

R_B t can be proven by: SN t ∧ (∀ t', step t t' → R_B t').  Is this equivalent to the definition? Actually SN t is defined inductively; SN t ↔ (∀ t', step t t' → SN t') with the caveat that the RHS implies SN t by SN_intro ✓. So yes.

  - ST_App1: impossible since abs doesn't step.

Let me think about the term ((λ.body) s) w more carefully, and prove R_D of it by yet another induction. Structure: it's an application with function part f = (λ.body) s and argument w. Reducts:
  - f steps: either s steps (ST_App2) or beta (s value) → open body s. In both cases, the reduct of the whole term is (f' w) for f' a reduct of f. And we know f' ∈ R_B?? Alternatively, let's define R_{A→B} in a "curried application" way that handles this:

Insight: The statement we actually need for the fundamental theorem's application case is R_B(t1 t2) where t1 ∈ R_{A→B}, t2 ∈ R_A. So Lemma K (abstraction applied to reducible arg) is the crux. Let's focus entirely on it:

**K: ∀ A B body s, lc_at 1 body? (or lc(abs)) → (∀ u, R_A u → R_B (open body u)) → R_A s → R_B (app (abs A body) s).**

Hmm, note: the hypothesis is about *open body u* for all u ∈ R_A. But for the beta step we need s to be a *value*. So maybe the hypothesis should be restricted to values: (∀ v, value v → R_A v → R_B (open body v)). Then for non-value s, the term (λ.body)s doesn't beta-reduce, only ST_App2 steps. Hmm, so maybe the value-restricted version is easier! Let's consider using the value-quantified LR:

R_Bool t := SN t
R_{A→B} t := SN t ∧ ∀ v, value v → R_A v → R_B (t v)

Then:
- Fundamental theorem's app case: t1 ∈ R_{A→B}, t2 ∈ R_A, show R_B(t1 t2). Now we need the "App lemma" for general t2. Hmm.
- Abstraction case: ∀ v value in R_A, R_B((λ.body) v): only need to handle value arguments. Reducts of (λ.body)v: beta → open body v ∈ R_B by typing IH ✓; ST_App2 impossible (v doesn't step); ST_App1 impossible. So SN((λ.body)v) from SN of the beta reduct ✓. And if B is an arrow: ∀w value in R_C, R_D((λ.body)v w): reducts of ((λ.body)v) w: ST_App1 if (λ.body)v --> ... — that happens if v is a value ✓ beta! (λ.body)v --> open body v. So ((λ.body)v) w --> (open body v) w. And R_D((open body v) w) from open body v ∈ R_{C→D}? Wait, open body v ∈ R_B = R_{C→D} ✓ (typing IH), and its arrow component gives ∀w value in R_C, R_D((open body v) w) ✓!!! 
  So ((λ.body)v) w has all reducts: (a) (open body v) w [beta on the left, allowed since v is a value] → reduced to R_D via open body v ∈ R_B ✓; (b) if v --> v' (v is a value, so no); (c) w steps: requires (λ.body)v to be a value — it's not. So all reducts of ((λ.body)v) w are of the form (f' w) where f' ∈ R_B, and f' ∈ R_B gives R_D(f' w) for w ∈ R_C ✓.
  So with **value arguments only**, everything works beautifully! Because the only "external" reductions are the beta on the function part, and every reduct of the function part is in R_B, whose arrow component handles the argument.

This is the classic CBV Girard-Tait proof! Great. So the value-quantified definition is the way.

Now the remaining crux: the fundamental theorem's application case with the value-quantified LR: need **App lemma**: t1 ∈ R_{A→B}, t2 ∈ R_A → R_B(t1 t2) for arbitrary t2 (not necessarily a value).

Proof: by induction on the type B? Let's think with the value-quantified definition:
- B = Bool: need SN(t1 t2). Prove by nested induction: outer on SN t1, inner on SN t2.

But wait, the outer IH statement needs to be "∀t2, R_A t2 → R_B(t1' t2)" and the inner SN-induction on t2 is happening *while* proving SN(t1 t2). Hmm, need to be careful with the induction structure.     Claim: ∀ t1, SN t1 → R_{A→B} t1 → ∀ t2, R_A t2 → SN (t1 t2).   [outer induction on SN t1]
    Proof: intros t1 Hsn1 HR1 t2 HR2. induction Hsn1. So IH: ∀ t1', t1 --> t1' → R_{A→B} t1' → ∀ t2, R_A t2 → SN (t1' t2).

    Hmm. So for the arrow case we need: every reduct of (t1 t2) is in R_B, and then R_D((t1 t2) v) — but we need R_B(t1 t2) itself to conclude, which is the goal. Circular? We can prove this by the *induction hypothesis at type D* if we can express (t1 t2) v as an application of an R-function to an R-argument: it's `f v` with f = t1 t2 and v ∈ R_C. Let me try to prove the App lemma at type B by induction on B with the *statement including a value-argument*:

Actually, better: generalize the App lemma to handle a **list of arguments**, or reformulate:

**Lemma App' : ∀ A B t1 t2, R_{A→B} t1 → R_A t2 → R_B (t1 t2).**

Try proving by induction on B with a nested SN-induction. In the arrow case B = C→D, we need ∀ v ∈ V_C, R_D((t1 t2) v). Note: (t1 t2) v = app (app t1 t2) v. Hmm.

New idea: instead of proving R_D((t1 t2) v) from scratch, use the **IH of the induction on B at type D with a different function**: We know from R_{A→B} t1 = R_{A→(C→D)} t1 that ∀ t2 ∈ R_A, R_{C→D}(t1 t2) — this is precisely the arrow component with t2. But that's what we're proving (App at B).

Hmm, wait!!! Hold on. R_{A→B} t1 where B = C→D is: SN t1 ∧ ∀ v value ∈ V_A, R_B(t1 v). So the arrow component only gives R_B(t1 v) for *values* v. And the App lemma extends this to non-values. For values it's given.

So the App lemma for B = C→D: given R_{A→B} t1 and R_A t2, prove R_B(t1 t2) = SN(t1 t2) ∧ ∀v ∈ V_C, R_D((t1 t2) v).
The second component: for each value v ∈ R_C, the term (t1 t2) v. Hmm.

Since t2 ∈ R_A and (if we're luckier) t2 is a value, we'd get t1 t2 ∈ R_B directly from the arrow component. But t2 arbitrary.

Let's use the SN-induction on t2 in the App lemma and the structure of reducts. Note that in R_D((t1 t2) v), the only thing that matters is the behavior of (t1 t2) as a function. Formally, we might prove:

**Lemma (functional app closure):** If SN t and (∀ t', step t t' → R_{C→D}???) hmm.

Alternatively, maybe there's a cleverer LR definition that makes everything work: **quantify over values in arrow type, and prove the App lemma via the "value" restriction by showing that any R_A term is "equivalent" to values in the sense that its applications are reducible** — the "backward closure" idea:

**Backward closure (general):** If t -->* v (multi-step to a value) hmm.

Actually here's the thing: in CBV, R_A terms can be stuck non-values (like fvar x). For those, t1 t2 is also stuck (if t1 is stuck too), hence SN and its applications are also stuck → reducible? Let's check: if t1 = fvar x (stuck), t2 ∈ R_A arbitrary: (fvar x) t2 — reducts: ST_App1 needs fvar x --> (impossible), ST_App2 needs fvar x to be a value (no), ST_AppAbs no. So (fvar x) t2 is SN ✓. For arrow result B = C→D: ((fvar x) t2) v: reducts? The function part (fvar x) t2 is stuck (not a value), so only ST_App1 on it → impossible. So ((fvar x)t2)v is stuck too, for any v! And iterated applications (((fvar x) t2) v) w... all stuck. So SN ✓ at all depths. So the App lemma holds for stuck functions trivially, and the interesting case is when t1 is an abstraction or reduces to one.

So the proof of the App lemma should be by induction on the SN derivation of t1 and t2 with the "applications" handled by an auxiliary statement about iterated applications. Let's formalize the needed statement:

**Key aux lemma:** For all types A, B, terms t1 t2, if R_{A→B} t1 and R_A t2, then R_B (t1 t2).

Since types are finite, the number of arguments is bounded by the type. This is the standard approach for "hereditary" definitions.

OK here's another thought. What if we define the LR differently: **accumulate arguments**. Define R_B t for B = B1→...→Bn→Bool as: SN t ∧ (∀ v1 ∈ R_{B1}, SN (t v1)) ∧ (∀ v1 ∈ R_{B1}, ∀ v2 ∈ R_{B2}, SN (t v1 v2)) ∧ ... ∧ (∀ v1..vn, SN (t v1 ... vn)). Then:
- The abstraction case: (λx.body) s with s ∈ R_A: need to show all prefix applications SN. This requires showing SN of (λ.body) s v1 ... vk. By nested SN-induction on s (and the beta step) plus the typing IH. Hmm, this seems more tractable because we're only ever proving SN statements, never R statements at the same type!
- The application case: t1 ∈ R_{A→B}, t2 ∈ R_A gives R_B(t1 t2): the conditions for R_B(t1 t2) are: SN(t1 t2), SN(t1 t2 v1), ..., which are exactly the conditions for R_{A→B}(t1) with the argument list (t2, v1, ..., vk) — provided t2 ∈ R_A ✓. 

So the "prefix SN" characterization makes the application case trivial! And the abstraction case reduces to proving SN of prefix applications, which we can prove by nested induction on the SN derivations of the arguments plus the typing IH.

Let me define this carefully. For a type B, define the set of "argument lists":
```
Fixpoint LR (B : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match B with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow B1 B2 => SN t ∧ (forall v, LR B1 v -> LR B2 (app t v))
  end.
```
This is the same as before. But let's *prove* the characterization lemma:

`LR_char : forall B t, LR B t <-> SN t ∧ forall args Ts?? ...`

Hmm, actually maybe simpler: prove the App lemma directly using an auxiliary notion. Let me define:

```
Fixpoint SN_apps (B : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match B with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow B1 B2 => SN t /\ forall v, LR B1 v -> SN_apps B2 (app t v)
  end.
```
Then prove LR B t ↔ SN_apps B t by induction on B:
- Bool: refl.
- Arrow B1 B2: LR = SN t ∧ ∀v ∈ R_{B1}, LR B2 (t v); SN_apps = SN t ∧ ∀v ∈ R_{B1}, SN_apps B2 (t v). By IH at B2, LR B2 (t v) ↔ SN_apps B2 (t v) ✓. But wait: the IH needs to be applied to the term (t v) — IH is `∀ t, LR B2 t ↔ SN_apps B2 t` ✓ (generalize over t).
Great, so LR ↔ SN_apps.

But is SN_apps easier to work with? SN_apps B t = SN t ∧ ∀v1 ∈ R_{B1}, SN(t v1) ∧ ∀v2 ∈ R_{B2}, SN(t v1 v2) ∧ ... — i.e., all prefix applications are SN. The application case: LR (A→B) t1 and LR A t2 → LR B (t1 t2). Via the characterization: need SN_apps B (t1 t2). Unfold: SN(t1 t2) ∧ ∀v1 ∈ R_{B1}, SN(t1 t2 v1) ∧ ... And the corresponding facts from SN_apps (A→B) t1 = LR (A→B) t1: SN t1 ∧ ∀u ∈ R_A, SN_apps B (t1 u), applied at u := t2 ✓ gives exactly SN_apps B (t1 t2)! That's immediate. 

And the abstraction case: LR (A→B) (λ.body) where B = B1→...→Bn→Bool. Need:
- SN(λ.body) ✓.
- ∀v1 ∈ R_{B1}, SN((λ.body) v1) ∧ ∀v2 ∈ R_{B2}, SN((λ.body) v1 v2) ∧ ...
So we need: all prefix applications of (λ.body) are SN, for reducible arguments. Proof: by induction on the number/types of arguments (i.e., on B), with nested SN-induction on the arguments? Let's see.

Let me state a general lemma:

**Lemma SN_apps_abs:** For all B, body, t (t = application of λ.body to some args, or just start with the abstraction itself), if ... 

Let's define: `abs_apps body args := (λ.body) applied to args`. We want SN (abs_apps body args) given:
- lc(abs body),
- LR args at their types (each vi ∈ R_{Bi}),
- typing: ∀ u ∈ R_A, LR B (open body u).

Proof by induction on... the term structure? Let's think about the reducts of `(λ.body) args`:
- If all args are values and there are n args: beta on the head → open body v1 applied to v2..vn. Then SN of that. Hmm, but open body v1 is not an abstraction necessarily! It's an arbitrary term with type B. Hmm. But we know LR B (open body v1) (from typing IH at v1 ∈ R_A), and LR B → SN_apps B, so all prefix applications of (open body v1) are SN, in particular SN((open body v1) v2 ... vn) ✓.
- If some argument steps or the head part steps: reduce to shorter arguments or reduced args.

So we need an induction on the SN derivations of the arguments (and, when the head isn't yet an abstraction, on the SN derivation of the head). This is the standard "SN of application towers" argument. Let me think about how to formalize.

Let me define the notion: `apps t args` (left-nested application). Reducts of `apps (abs body) args`:
For a left-nested application `apps t [a1; a2; ...; an]` in CBV:
- if t is not a value: a step must occur in t (ST_App1/App2 at the innermost level: `t a1` steps if t steps (needs lc a1), or if t is a value and a1 steps). So the step is in the head `t` or in the first non-value... Actually more precisely, evaluation is left-to-right: the term is `(...((t a1) a2)... an)`. A step happens at some level k where all of t, a1, ..., a_{k-1} are values and the "active" part steps:
  - ST_AppAbs at level 1: t = abs, a1 value → reduces to `apps (open body a1) [a2...an]`.
  - ST_App1/2 at some level.
  
To prove SN of the whole tower, we do induction on the SN derivations of the components. But when the head is an abstraction and a1 is a value, the beta step gives `apps (open body a1) [a2..an]` — and its SN needs to come from the *typing hypothesis* (LR B (open body a1) → SN_apps B), not from the SN of the components. So the induction structure is: we prove SN(apps (abs body) args) by SN_intro on all reducts; each reduct is either a tower with fewer args (handled by typing/LR), or a tower with a reduced component (handled by IH on the SN derivation of that component).

The cleanest formalization: generalized induction on the list of arguments with their SN derivations, i.e., a lemma:

`Lemma SN_apps_abs : forall B body (args : list tm) (Ts : list ty), LR_args Ts args -> (forall u, LR A u -> LR B (open body u)) -> SN (apps (abs A body) args)`

where apps with the args' types. The induction would be on... the combined SN derivations of args. Since we need to induct on a list of SN derivations, we can use a well-founded measure or induction on the list with nested inductions. Alternatively use `Acc` with a lexicographic ordering. Hmm, this is getting complicated in Coq.

**Alternative: use `SN` defined as `Acc step` (well-founded accessibility)!** Then we can do well-founded induction on terms directly, which is much more flexible than the inductive SN definition. But the target theorem uses the given inductive `strongly_normalizing`. We can prove `strongly_normalizing t ↔ Acc step t` (easy: both directions by induction) and then use Acc for the proof. Hmm, but the LR definition uses SN; we could define LR using Acc and then convert at the end. That gives us well-founded induction on terms which handles the "application tower" induction more naturally (induction on the SN derivations of the components = well-founded induction on the components). **Robust approach: use `Acc` (well-foundedness) for everything.**

Given: `strongly_normalizing t` is an inductive Prop. Prove `strongly_normalizing t ↔ Acc step t`. Then all SN reasoning can use Acc's well-founded induction, which gives us much more flexibility (e.g., induction on the multiset of SN derivations of multiple arguments via lexicographic combinations or `Acc` on a product).

But the fundamental theorem's abstraction case needs a nested induction on the argument's SN with a statement about the term. With Acc, we can do induction on `Acc step s` for the argument s, but the problems with the arrow-result type remain (needing to handle towers).

Let me think about the tower problem more carefully to find the cleanest formulation.

**Towers:** The fundamental theorem's abstraction case needs: for B arbitrary, ∀s ∈ R_A, R_B((λ.body)s). Unfolding R_B for B = B1→...→Bn→Bool: for all v1..vn ∈ R_{B1..Bn}: SN((((λ.body)s) v1) ... vn) and intermediate prefix SNs (which are implied by SN at the full length? No: R_B(prefix) requires SN of prefixes; but if SN(full application), does SN(prefix) follow? Yes! SN(t v) → SN(t) given lc v: because steps of t produce steps of t v. So SN of the full tower implies SN of all prefixes ✓).

So it suffices to prove: `∀ v1..vn ∈ R, SN(apps (abs A body) (s :: v1 :: ... :: vn))` — i.e., SN of the full tower. And the typing hypothesis gives `∀ u ∈ R_A, R_B(open body u)` → `∀ u ∈ R_A, ∀v1..vn ∈ R, SN(apps (open body u) [v1..vn])`.

So the key lemma is:

**Lemma T:** For all A, B, body, and lists of terms `args` with types `As` (where the tower is (λ: A, body) applied to args), if
  (i) lc(abs A body),
  (ii) ∀ u ∈ R_A, R_B(open body u)  [i.e., the body is semantically typed],
  (iii) each arg ∈ R at its type,
then SN(apps (abs A body) args) — where B is the type of the result after applying all args (B = the type of `apps (abs A body) args`).

Proof: by induction on B? Or by induction on the arguments' SN derivations?

Let's think about what reducts look like. Define `apps t args`. If `args = []`: term is `abs A body`, a value, SN ✓.
If args = a1 :: rest:
- The term is `apps (app (abs A body) a1) rest` = `(λ.body) a1` applied to rest.
- Steps: 
  - beta at the head: if a1 is a value: `open body a1` applied to rest — SN from hypothesis (ii) at a1 ∈ R_A: R_B(open body a1) → SN_apps → SN(apps (open body a1) rest) ✓ (R_B(open body a1) unfolded to the full tower).
  - step in a1 (requires abs to be a value ✓ always): a1 --> a1', giving `(λ.body) a1'` applied to rest: use IH with a1' ∈ R_A (forward closure) and SN a1' (decreased) ✓.
  - step in rest: requires the head `(λ.body) a1` to be a value — it's an application, so not a value → no.
  Wait, careful: the term `apps (abs A body) (a1 :: rest)` = `(((abs A body) a1) a2) ... an`. A step can occur anywhere along the spine:
    - At the innermost app `(abs A body) a1`: beta (if a1 value) or ST_App2 (a1 --> a1'). ✓ handled.
    - At the next level `((abs A body) a1) a2`: requires the function `(abs A body) a1` to be a value (for ST_App2) — it's not — or for ST_App1 the function steps ✓ handled above (the reduct of the function part).
    - In general, a step at level k > 1 requires the function part (a prefix application) to be a value — never (applications aren't values) — or ST_App1 with the function part stepping, which recursively is a step of the prefix application.
  So actually the only *immediate* steps are at the innermost level (level 1), i.e., the head `(abs A body) a1`! Because for ST_App2 we need the function to be a value and prefix applications are never values; for ST_App1 the function steps, and recursively the step is at level 1. So all steps reduce the innermost application. 
  
  Hmm wait, that means a tower `((f a1) a2)` where f a1 --> f' a1 gives `(f' a1) a2` — yes, that's a step in the prefix. So inductively all steps occur at level 1 (the innermost). But careful: after several steps, the head could become a value? No: applications never become... well, `(abs A body) a1` could reduce to `open body a1`, which could be anything, e.g., a value if body = bvar 0! E.g., body = bvar 0, then `(λx.x) v --> v` — a value, and then the tower `((λx.x) v) a2 --> v a2` can continue stepping at level 2! Hmm. So my claim "steps only at level 1" is wrong in general: after the head reduces to a value, higher-level steps become possible. But wait, in the reduction `((λx.x) v) a2 --> v a2` — this IS a step at level 1 (the innermost app `(λx.x) v --> v`), producing `v a2`, which then can step at level 2.

  So the SN measure must account for: the head part reducing can expose a value, enabling further steps. This is exactly why the typing hypothesis (ii) is crucial: when the head becomes `open body v`, we know from typing that the resulting tower is SN.

So the induction for the tower lemma: prove SN(apps (abs A body) args) — with the hypothesis (ii) that for all u ∈ R_A, R_B(open body u) (giving SN of the tower after beta at any depth? Let's see: R_B(open body u) gives all prefix applications of `open body u` are SN, for ALL argument lists ✓).

Now the induction: SN(apps (abs A body) args) via SN_intro. Each reduct t':
- Case beta at level 1: t' = apps (open body a1) rest, SN from (ii) applied to a1 ∈ R_A: R_B(open body a1) → SN_apps → SN of the tower. But hold on: is R_B(open body a1) giving SN of `apps (open body a1) rest` where rest has types matching B's arguments? Yes: R_B is the LR at type B = type of the full tower, and SN_apps B t gives SN(apps t args) for all args of the right types... Well, in the formalization we need to state the correspondence. Let's just define the lemma with the list of argument types as an explicit parameter.
- Case step at a deeper level: t' = apps (abs A body) args' where the head is unchanged and one arg changed? No — from the analysis: a step at level k > 1 requires the prefix (level k-1) to step (ST_App1), which recursively means a step at level 1. Hmm, no: ST_App1 at level k: function part is `apps (abs A body) (a1..a_{k-1})`, argument a_k, and the premise is the function part steps with lc(a_k) ✓. So the step could be at level k-1, etc. But ultimately some level j steps and all levels above just apply ST_App1 with unchanged args. So the reduct t' = apps (abs A body) [a1..a_{j-1}, a_j', a_{j+1}..] where a_j --> a_j' via a level-1 step, with all levels above being ST_App1.

  Hmm, so a step of the tower is: choose j, and the j-th arg steps (a_j --> a_j', with the requirement that the prefix function `apps (abs A body) [a1..a_{j-1}]` is locally closed? ST_App1 requires lc of the argument, not the function. Let me re-read ST_App1: `t1 --> t1'`, `locally_closed t2`, `tm_app t1 t2 --> tm_app t1' t2`. **Definitions:**
```
Fixpoint SN_apps (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ forall v, LR T1 v -> SN_apps T2 (tm_app t v)

```
Hmm, mutual Fixpoints (SN_apps and LR are the same thing!). Indeed LR with the all-terms definition IS SN_apps. And the characterization lemma I wanted (LR ↔ prefix SN) is just the definition, with the observation that LR T t is equivalent to "SN of all prefix applications". So define:

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u)
  end.
```

Then:
- `LR B t` directly gives SN(t) and, for arrow, the application component. 
- The "tower SN" statement `SN(apps t args)` for reducible args follows by iterating the definition ✓ (provable by induction on args or directly).

OK, let me now seriously consider the standard structure that handles all this. I recall that in Coq developments of STLC normalization (e.g., in the "Metatheory" libraries or the LN tutorial), the key lemma is often stated as:

Actually, hold on! Let me reconsider. In the LN tutorial by Charguéraud ("Lecture notes on the locally nameless representation"), Section 4 "Strong normalization of the simply-typed lambda-calculus" — I'm now fairly confident this exists with:

```
Fixpoint red (T:typ) (t:trm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | typ_bool => sn t
  | typ_arrow T1 T2 => 
      sn t /\ (forall v, value v -> red T1 v -> red T2 (trm_app t v))
  end.
```

2. `LR T t → lc t` (by definition)
3. `LR T t → t --> t' → LR T t'` (forward closure)
4. `(∀ t', t --> t' → LR T t') → LR T t` (backward closure) — **requires lc t**? and SN t?

**Resolution attempt:** Strengthen the statement to include "∀v ∈ LR_C (not just values), LR_D(t v)". That's the all-terms version. Then the abstraction case needs the tower argument (since beta only fires on values).

Hmm, we're going in circles. Let me think about which formulation minimizes total work. I think the "all-terms" LR with a proper tower lemma is the way, since applications to arbitrary reducible args are exactly what the application case needs, and the abstraction case's tower argument can be done with the SN-induction on the first argument (which we showed is sound because all tower steps happen at the head).

Let me now carefully lay out the full proof with the all-terms LR:

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => lc t /\ SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

**Lemma 1 (LR_lc, LR_SN):** LR T t → lc t, SN t. (By induction on T / direct for arrow, Bool direct.)

**Lemma 2 (forward closure):** LR T t → step t t' → LR T t'.
By induction on T.
- Bool: lc t' (from lc t via lc_step_forward), SN t' (from SN t via SN_step) ✓.
- Arrow T1 T2: lc t' ✓, SN t' ✓, ∀u ∈ LR T1, LR T2(t' u). We have LR T2(t u) from the arrow component. And t u --> t' u by ST_App1 (needs lc u ✓ from LR T1 u). Then IH at T2 (structural, T2 is a subterm? Note: LR T2 is a subterm of LR (T1→T2) — for the Fixpoint induction, the induction principle generated gives the hypothesis for T1 and T2 ✓) gives LR T2(t' u) ✓.
✓

**Lemma 3 (backward closure):** LR T t' → step t t' → LR T t.
By induction on T.
- Bool: lc t: hmm, backward lc. Counterexample found earlier: t = app (abs A (abs A' (bvar 0))) (bvar 3), t' = abs A' (bvar 0)? Let's check: open_rec 0 (bvar 3) (abs A' (bvar 0)) = abs A' (open_rec 1 (bvar 3) (bvar 0)) = abs A' (bvar 0) ✓ lc_at 0 ✓. But t = app (abs A (abs A' (bvar 0))) (bvar 3): lc requires lc(bvar 3) which fails. So lc t' does NOT imply lc t. So backward closure at Bool fails without extra hypotheses!

  Hmm! So LR_Bool isn't backward-closed. Unless we add hypotheses: e.g., lc t (which we might have from context). So state backward closure with an lc hypothesis: `lc t → LR T t' → step t t' → LR T t`. Then for Bool: lc ✓, SN t from SN t' + determinism ✓ (or from the fact that the only reduct is t'). ✓
- Arrow T1 T2: lc t ✓ given, SN t ✓ (determinism), ∀u ∈ LR T1, LR T2(t u): have LR T2(t' u) and t u --> t' u (ST_App1 with lc u ✓). Need lc(t u) ✓ (from lc t, lc u) and then IH at T2 gives LR T2(t u) ✓.
✓ Good, with lc in the hypotheses.

**Lemma 4 (abs case):** LR A u → (∀u, LR A u → LR B (open body u)) → lc(abs A body) → LR B (app (abs A body) u). 

This is the tower problem. With the all-terms LR, let's use backward closure! We have u ∈ LR A → SN u → and (app abs u) --> open body u when u is a value. If u is not a value, hmm.

Let me split: 
- If u is a value: (app abs u) --> open body u, and LR B(open body u) ✓ from the hypothesis; backward closure at B (needs lc(app abs u) ✓) gives LR B(app abs u) ✓.
- If u is not a value: then (app abs u) has reducts only via ST_App2 (u --> u'), i.e., (app abs u'). So we do induction on SN u: 
  - Goal LR B(app abs u), by backward closure... hmm, but backward closure needs a reduct. If u is not a value and has no reducts (stuck), then (app abs u) is stuck (no reducts at all), and we need to prove LR B of a stuck term.
  For a stuck term t = (app abs u) with u stuck non-value: LR B t for B = Bool: lc ✓, SN ✓ (no reducts). For B = C→D: need ∀v ∈ LR C, LR D(t v) — the tower with a stuck head: (t v) is stuck (t not a value → only ST_App1 which needs t to step ✓ none). So recursively, towers with stuck heads have no reducts → SN ✓, lc ✓, and deeper towers ✓. So LR B(t) holds for stuck towers.
  
  So we need a lemma: **stuck towers are reducible**. Hmm, and "stuck" here means: the head is not a value and has no reducts. But in the general case, u might have reducts. So the induction on SN u handles the reduct case, and the stuck case needs the lemma.

Hmm, but there's a subtlety: when u is not a value and has reducts u', we get LR B(app abs u') from IH (with LR A u' from forward closure ✓), and then LR B(app abs u) by backward closure at B (needs lc ✓ — lc(app abs u) from lc abs, lc u ✓). But backward closure requires a reduct of (app abs u) → (app abs u') ✓ exists.
When u is a value: beta reduct + backward closure ✓.
When u is stuck non-value: no reducts → need the "stuck tower" lemma.

So I need a notion of stuck/neutral. Let's define:

```
Definition neutral (t : tm) : Prop := 
  match t with
  | tm_app t1 t2 => neutral t1 \/ (value t1 /\ neutral t2)  (* hmm *)
  ...
```
Hmm, maybe simpler: define "no step": `irreducible t := forall t', ~ step t t'`. Then:
- A term with no reducts that is lc is in LR at every type? Let's check: t irreducible → SN t ✓ (no reducts, SN_intro vacuous). LR_Bool t = lc ∧ SN ✓. LR_{C→D} t = lc ∧ SN ∧ ∀v ∈ LR C, LR_D(t v). Better: restructure the App lemma to avoid the case split. Instead of "if it has a reduct", do case analysis on t1's structure:

Hmm, what determines whether t1 t2 has a reduct? Let's see:
- If t1 = tm_abs T body and t2 is a value: beta applies → reduct exists.
- If t1 = tm_abs T body and t2 is not a value: a reduct exists iff t2 has a reduct.
- If t1 is not a value: a reduct exists iff t1 has a reduct.
- If t1 is a value but not an abs (true/false): a reduct exists iff t2 has a reduct.
So "no reduct" ⟺ (t1 not a value and t1 irreducible) or (t1 value and t2 irreducible and not (t1 abs ∧ t2 value)).

Rather than a case split on existence, do a **mutual/nested well-founded induction on SN of the components**, handling all reducts constructively:

**App lemma (final structure):**
```
Lemma LR_app : forall A B t1 t2, LR (A→B) t1 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (t1 t2).
Proof.
  intros A B t1 t2 H1 H2.
  (* get SN *)
  pose proof (LR_SN _ _ H1) as S1. pose proof (LR_SN _ _ H2) as S2.
  revert t1 H1 A B S1.
  (* induction on SN t2 *)
```

  - t1 = abs T body: if t2 is a value: beta ✓ (LR from arrow component + backward closure). If t2 is not a value: (t1 t2) reduces only via ST_App2 with t2 --> t2'. So we need to handle t2's reducts — induct on SN t2. But then the "t2 irreducible non-value" case: (abs body t2) is stuck → LR_irred ✓ (t2 irreducible non-value; abs is a value, so (app abs t2) steps only via ST_AppAbs (needs value t2 ✗) or ST_App2 (needs t2 --> ✗)).
  - t1 = true/false: (t1 t2) steps only via ST_App2 → induct on SN t2; irreducible → LR_irred ✓.
- t1 is not a value and not irreducible: t1 --> t1' → induct on SN t1; also the reduct (t1' t2) is LR by IH1 ✓. Then LR_B(t1 t2) by backward closure ✓.
- t1 is not a value and irreducible: handled by LR_irred ✓.

This is a case analysis on t1's structure plus "∃ step" for non-values, and on t2's value-ness. In Coq, "t1 is not a value and not irreducible" requires a case split on ∃t', step t1 t' — again classical. Hmm. Alternatively, case analysis on the *shape* of t1:
- tm_bvar n (not a value, irreducible ✓ → LR_irred)
- tm_fvar x (✓ LR_irred)
- tm_app t11 t12 (not a value; irreducible iff no step... hmm, this recurses)
- tm_abs (value)
- tm_true/false (values)

The tm_app case requires knowing whether it steps. Recursion on the structure of t1 could work: if t1 = app t11 t12 and it's in LR (A→B), then... hmm, it's an app; is there a step? Depends on t11.

OK, let me just consider using **classical logic**. Is that acceptable? The task says: "must not use `Admitted`, added axioms, unsafe flags". Using `Classical` from the standard library introduces the axiom `classic` (which is `Classical_Prop.classic : forall P, P \/ ~P`, itself derived from `classic` axiom or from `ClassicalDescription`...). **Alternative: prove decidability of step.** Step is a decidable relation! Because it's defined by inductive rules on a decidable syntactic structure, with premises that are decidable (`locally_closed` is decidable, `value` is decidable). We could prove `forall t t', decidable (step t t')` by induction on t. That's a chore but doable. Hmm.

**Alternative: avoid the case split entirely.** Here's a thought: instead of "if no reducts then LR_irred", we can prove:

`LR_B(t1 t2)` by **induction on the structure of the SN derivation of t1 and t2**, with the statement covering all cases, and handle the "stuck" scenario via a different formulation:

Actually, here's a cleaner idea: prove the **contrapositive-ish** characterization: LR_T t ⟺ all reducts are in LR_T AND t is "well-formed". Define:

`LR T t := SN t ∧ lc t ∧ ∀ t', step t t' → LR T t'` — coinductive definition! Prove it's equivalent to the Fixpoint definition, then use the coinductive one with SN-induction. Hmm, but is the equivalence true? Forward: LR → all reducts LR ✓ (forward closure), SN ✓, lc ✓. Backward: SN t ∧ lc t ∧ ∀ reducts LR → LR T t. Prove by induction on SN t... for T = C→D: need ∀v ∈ LR C, LR_D(t v). By induction on SN t and the SN of v? We need SN(t v), which needs the typed structure... circular again? **Decidable step:** Let me think about the cost. Define `step_dec : forall t t', {step t t'} + {~ step t t'}`. Approach: characterize by structural recursion on t:
- t = bvar/fvar/true/false: no steps (unless true/false... true --> t'? no rules ✓). So `right; intro H; inversion H`.
- t = abs T body: no steps ✓.
- t = app t1 t2: 
  - if t1 = abs T body and value t2: step is possible (ST_AppAbs) — but also, could t1 also step? t1 is an abs → no. Could t2 step? If value t2, no ✓. So the step is uniquely ST_AppAbs → `left; apply ST_AppAbs` ✓.
  - if t1 is not an abs (or t2 not a value): check if t1 steps: if yes → ST_App1 (needs lc t2! Hmm, ST_App1 requires locally_closed t2 — if t2 isn't lc, no step via ST_App1). Hmm, this complicates.
  - if t1 is a value and t2 steps → ST_App2 ✓.
  - if t1 steps and lc t2 → ST_App1 ✓.
  - if t1 steps and not lc t2 and t1 not a value: hmm, both ST_AppAbs (needs t1 abs ✗) and ST_App2 (needs t1 value ✗) fail, so no step. So no step ✓.
  - Combinations require checking value t1, value t2, lc t2, and step t1 / step t2.
  
  So I'd need decision procedures for `value`, `lc`, and `step` mutually recursive. Doable but a bit of work. Since `value` and `lc` are simple structural predicates, and step is recursive on subterms... The mutual recursion is on the term's structure: `step (app t1 t2)` depends on `step t1` and `step t2` (subterms) ✓, so it's structurally recursive ✓. We'd define:
  
  ```
  Fixpoint step_dec (t : tm) : forall t', {step t t'} + {~step t t'} :=
    match t with
    | bvar/fvar/true/false => fun t' => right (no_step)
    | abs _ _ => fun t' => right (no_step)
    | app t1 t2 => fun t' =>
       ... use step_dec t1, step_dec t2, value_dec t1, value_dec t2, lc_dec t2 ...
  ```
  But `step_dec t1` has type `forall t', {step t1 t'} + ...` — using it to decide "∃ t', step t1 t'" requires... Hmm, we can't decide existence from a `forall` decision procedure directly without knowing the reduct. Hmm! To decide whether t1 steps, we'd need to search over all t'. Not directly structural.
  
  Better approach: define a **deterministic step function** `stepf : tm -> option tm` that computes the reduct, and prove `step t t' ↔ stepf t = Some t'`. Then decidability follows. Since step is deterministic and syntax-directed, this is very doable:
  
  ```
  Fixpoint stepf (t : tm) : option tm :=
    match t with
    | tm_app t1 t2 =>
        match t1 with
        | tm_abs T body => match value_dec t2 with
                           | left _ => Some (open body t2)   (* but need lc(abs) too! *)
                           | right _ => match stepf t2 with Some t2' => Some (app t1 t2') | None => None end
                           end
        ...
  ```
  Hmm, the ST_AppAbs rule also requires `locally_closed (tm_abs T t)`. And ST_App1 requires lc t2. So:
  
  ```
  stepf (app t1 t2) :=
    match t1 with
    | tm_abs T body => if lc_dec (tm_abs T body) then
                         if value_dec t2 then Some (open body t2)
                         else match stepf t2 with Some t2' => Some (app t1 t2') | None => None end
                       else (* t1 is an abs but not lc: can it step? ST_App1 requires t1 step (no), ST_App2 requires value t1 (yes! abs is a value only if lc!) *)
       ...
  ```
  Hmm, `value (tm_abs T body)` requires `locally_closed (tm_abs T body)` ✓ per the v_abs constructor. So if the abs isn't lc, it's not a value. Then (app abs t2) can only step via ST_AppAbs (needs lc ✗) or ST_App2 (needs value t1 ✗) or ST_App1 (needs t1 step ✗). So no step.
  
  This is getting messy but is mechanical. Hmm, is there a simpler way to decide "t1 steps"?

  | tm_false => None
  | tm_abs _ _ => None
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match t1, t2 with
      | tm_abs T body, _ => 
          if lc_dec (tm_abs T body) then
            if value_dec t2 then Some (open body t2)
            else match stepf t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2') | None => None end
          else None
      | _, _ =>
          match stepf t1 with
          | Some t1' => if lc_dec t2 then Some (tm_app t1' t2) else None
          | None => 
              if value_dec t1 then (match stepf t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2') | None => None) else None
          end
      end
  end
```

  - ST_App2: value t1, t2 --> t2'.

  - ST_AppAbs + ST_App1: requires t1 = abs and t1 --> t1': but abs is a value → no step ✓ (needs the lemma).
  - ST_AppAbs + ST_App2: requires value t2 and t2 --> t2': values don't step ✓.
  - ST_App1 + ST_App2: requires t1 --> t1' and value t1: values don't step ✓.
  So at most one applies ✓ (given determinism of sub-steps). Good, so the priority order doesn't matter semantically, but I'd need to prove the function correct against the relation (`step t t' ↔ stepf t = Some t'`), by induction on t, using inversion lemmas. ```
Fixpoint reduces (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ | tm_fvar _ | tm_true | tm_false | tm_abs _ _ => false
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_abs _ body => if (lcb (tm_abs ...)) && (valueb t2) then true else reduces t2
      ...
```
Hmm, but the correct semantics: 
`reduces (app t1 t2) = true` iff (t1 = abs ∧ lc t1 ∧ value t2) ∨ (reduces t1 ∧ lc t2) ∨ (value t1 ∧ reduces t2)`.

And then prove: `reduces t = true ↔ exists t', step t t'` and `reduces t = false ↔ forall t', ~ step t t'`. And also a "stepf" giving the actual reduct would be nice for the reduct analysis... but actually, the reduct analysis in the App lemma via inversion on the step hypothesis doesn't need stepf. I only need the *existence* decision to split cases (stuck vs reducible). 

Hmm, wait. Let's reconsider: do I need the existence decision, or would a *disjunction* lemma suffice: `(exists t', step t t') \/ (forall t', ~ step t t')`. Let me write the boolean function. Define:
```
Fixpoint valueb (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_abs T body => lcb (tm_abs T body)
  | tm_true | tm_false => true
  | _ => false
  end
with lcb (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => i <? 0  (* lc_at 0 *)
  ...
```
Careful: lc_at k with k as a parameter:
```
Fixpoint lcb_at (k : nat) (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => Nat.ltb i k
  | tm_fvar _ => true
  | tm_app t1 t2 => andb (lcb_at k t1) (lcb_at k t2)
  | tm_abs _ body => lcb_at (S k) body
  | tm_true | tm_false => true
  end.
```
And prove `lcb_at k t = true ↔ lc_at k t` by induction on t (with k generalized) ✓. Similarly `valueb t = true ↔ value t` (induction on t; the abs case uses lcb ✓).

Then:
```
Fixpoint reducesb (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => false
  | tm_fvar _ => false
  | tm_true => false
  | tm_false => false
  | tm_abs _ _ => false
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      if valueb t1 then
        (if (* t1 is an abs *) ... then valueb t2 else reducesb t2)
      else
        (reducesb t1 && lcb t2)
  end.
```
Hmm, more carefully:
- If t1 = tm_abs T body: step iff (lc(abs) ∧ value t2) [beta] ∨ (t2 steps ∧ value abs) [ST_App2 with value v1=abs — note ST_App2 requires value v1 = lc(abs)]. Since abs might not be lc, value(abs) = lc(abs) matters. So: reduces = (lc(abs) ∧ value t2) ∨ (lc(abs) ∧ reduces t2). Hmm: ST_App2 gives a step if value(abs) and t2 --> t2'. So reduces = lc(abs) ∧ (value t2 ∨ reduces t2). ✓
- If t1 ≠ abs:
  - ST_AppAbs: needs t1 abs ✗.
  - ST_App1: t1 --> t1' ∧ lc t2. Note: lc t2 is required. So reduces includes (reduces t1 ∧ lc t2).
  - ST_App2: value t1 ∧ t2 --> t2'. value t1 means t1 = abs (✗) or true/false.
  So: if t1 = true/false: reduces = value t1 ∧ reduces t2 = reduces t2.
  If t1 = bvar/fvar/true/false/app: reduces = (reduces t1 ∧ lc t2) ∨ (valueb t1 ∧ reduces t2).

So:
```
Fixpoint reducesb (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_abs T body => 
          if lcb (tm_abs T body) then (valueb t2 || reducesb t2) else false
      | _ =>
          (reducesb t1 && lcb t2) || (valueb t1 && reducesb t2)
      end
  | _ => false
  end
```
And prove: `reducesb t = true ↔ exists t', step t t'`. Proof by induction on t (structurally; the app case uses the IH for t1 and t2 ✓). This is doable but requires care with inversion on step.

Hmm, this is a decent amount of work (maybe 80-150 lines). Is there a cheaper way?

8. Fundamental theorem:
   ```
   Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> 
     forall rho, env_sem Gamma rho -> LR T (subst rho t).
   ```
   By induction on the typing derivation.
   - T_Var: Gamma x = Some T → env_sem gives rho x ∈ LR T; subst rho (fvar x) = rho x (with env_sem requiring lookup x rho = Some s). Since we use a list env: subst rho (tm_fvar x) = s when lookup x rho = Some s. So LR T (subst rho (fvar x)) ✓ needs the lemma `subst_fvar : lookup x rho = Some s → subst rho (fvar x) = s` ✓.
   - T_Abs: need LR (T1→T2) (subst rho (tm_abs T1 t1)) = LR (T1→T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst rho t1)). Components: lc ✓, SN ✓ (abs is a value), and ∀u ∈ LR T1, LR T2 (app (tm_abs T1 body') u) where body' = subst rho t1. Use LR_abs_app with hypothesis: ∀u ∈ LR T1, LR T2 (open body' u) = LR T2 (open (subst rho t1) u). And from the typing IH at the body `open t1 (fvar x)` with the extended environment rho' = (x, u) :: rho: LR T2 (subst rho' (open t1 (fvar x))) — and we need this to equal LR T2 (open (subst rho t1) u). So the substitution-opening lemma ✓ (with freshness conditions). 
   - T_App: t = app t1 t2 → subst rho t = app (subst rho t1) (subst rho t2); IH gives LR (T1→T2)(subst rho t1) and LR T1(subst rho t2); apply LR_app ✓.
   - T_True/T_False: LR Bool true = lc ✓ ∧ SN ✓; subst rho true = true ✓.
   
   For the T_Abs case, I need to choose the fresh x: x ∉ L, x ∉ FV(t1), x ∉ atoms(rho), x ∉ dom(rho)... and prove the substitution-opening lemma:
   `subst ((x,u)::rho) (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst rho t1) u` under the right freshness conditions.
   
   Hmm, and the env_sem for ((x,u)::rho) vs Gamma: we need env_sem (update Gamma x T1) ((x,u)::rho) given env_sem Gamma rho and LR T1 u. Let me define env_sem to make this easy:
   
   ```
   Definition env_sem (Gamma : context) (rho : env) : Prop :=
     (forall x s, env_lookup x rho = Some s -> locally_closed s) /\
     (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> exists s, env_lookup x rho = Some s /\ LR T s).
   ```
   Then env_sem (update Gamma x T1) ((x,u)::rho) given env_sem Gamma rho, LR T1 u:
   - lc: for y, s with lookup y ((x,u)::rho) = Some s: if y = x then s = u, lc u ✓ (LR → lc); else lookup y rho = Some s → lc ✓.
   - typing: for y, T with update Gamma x T1 y = Some T: if y = x then T = T1, need exists s, lookup x ((x,u)::rho) = Some s ∧ LR T1 s → take s = u ✓. Else Gamma y = Some T → exists s, lookup y rho = Some s ∧ LR T s → and lookup y ((x,u)::rho) = lookup y rho since y ≠ x ✓.
   ✓ Easy.

   And the freshness: x must not be in dom(rho) (as a key) and not in the fvars of rho's images, and not in FV(t1). Define:
   ```
   Fixpoint fv_list (t : tm) : list atom := ...
   Definition env_atoms (rho : env) : list atom := flat_map (fun p => p.1 :: fv_list (p.2)) rho.
   Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (fold_right Nat.max 0 L).
   Lemma fresh_spec : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
   Lemma fresh_notin : forall L x, In x L -> x < fresh L.  (* or fresh ∉ L *)
   ```
   
   Then in the T_Abs case, we instantiate the hypothesis at x := fresh (L ++ fv_list t1 ++ env_atoms rho). ✓

   Also need: subst doesn't change lc if rho's images are lc: `lc t → (forall x s, lookup x rho = Some s → lc s) → lc (subst rho t)` ✓.
   
   And the abstraction's lc: lc(tm_abs T1 (subst rho t1)) requires lc_at 1 (subst rho t1). Hmm! The lemma above gives lc_at 0 (subst rho t1) — but this is inside a binder, so we need lc_at 1. Since subst doesn't go under binders, lc_at is preserved... but the bvars in t1 at level 0 might be < 1; after subst, same bvars ✓. So the lemma should be stated for general k: `lc_at k t → (∀ images lc_at 0?) → lc_at k (subst rho t)`. Hmm, the images could contain bvars! If rho x = tm_bvar 0 (not lc), then substituting into a term at level k... but env_sem requires ALL images lc_at 0 ✓ (from LR → lc). Then lc_at k (subst rho t) for any k? Substituting a locally closed term for a fvar doesn't introduce bvars, so if t is lc_at k then subst rho t is lc_at k ✓ (as long as images are lc_at 0, which implies lc_at k for any k via monotonicity ✓). 
   
   So: `Lemma lc_subst: forall k t rho, lc_at k t -> (forall x s, env_lookup x rho = Some s -> lc_at k s) -> lc_at k (subst rho t)`. ✓ by induction on t (with k generalized).

   Hmm, and do we need lc of the ORIGINAL term t1 to prove lc_at 1 (subst rho t1)? Let me write the code.

```coq
(* ============ Part 1: basic syntactic infrastructure ============ *)

Definition SN : tm -> Prop := strongly_normalizing.

(* free variables list *)
Fixpoint fv_list (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv_list t1 ++ fv_list t2
  | tm_abs _ body => fv_list body
  | tm_true => []
  | tm_false => []
  end.
```

Environment:
```coq
Definition env := list (atom * tm).

Fixpoint env_lookup (x : atom) (rho : env) : option tm :=
  match rho with
  | [] => None
  | (y, s) :: rho' => if Nat.eqb x y then Some s else env_lookup x rho'
  end.

Fixpoint subst (rho : env) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar n => tm_bvar n
  | tm_fvar x => match env_lookup x rho with Some s => s | None => tm_fvar x end
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst rho t1) (subst rho t2)
  | tm_abs T body => tm_abs T (subst rho body)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Definition env_atoms (rho : env) : list atom :=
  flat_map (fun p => p.1 :: fv_list (p.2)) rho.

Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (fold_right Nat.max 0 L).
```

Freshness lemma:
```coq
Lemma fresh_gt : forall L x, In x L -> x < fresh L.
Proof.
  intros L. induction L as [| a L IH]; intros x H; [contradiction|].
  simpl in *. unfold fresh in *. simpl.
  destruct H as [-> | H].
  - lia.  (* a < S (max a (fold_right max 0 L)) *)
  - assert (x <= fold_right Nat.max 0 L) by (apply IH; auto).
    lia. (* need max a (...) >= fold_right ... *)
Qed.
```
Hmm, `fold_right Nat.max 0 (a :: L)` reduces to `Nat.max a (fold_right Nat.max 0 L)`; and x < S(max a rest) when x ≤ rest ≤ max a rest ✓ lia should handle with the bound x ≤ rest and rest ≤ max a rest. Need `rest <= Nat.max a rest` — lia can prove with Nat.le_max_r if we add it. Let me just do: `apply Nat.le_max_r` or `assert (fold_right Nat.max 0 L <= Nat.max a (fold_right Nat.max 0 L)) by apply Nat.le_max_r; lia`. Fine.

Then `fresh_notin : ~ In (fresh L) L` via fresh_gt with x = fresh L: if In (fresh L) L then fresh L < fresh L ✗ ✓.

LR Fixpoint:
```coq
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```
Hmm, this is a Fixpoint with a match inside a conjunction — should be fine syntactically. Let me test.

Hmm, one issue: `locally_closed` in the Bool case and arrow case both — fine.

Actually, maybe cleaner:
```coq
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => locally_closed t /\ SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => locally_closed t /\ SN t /\ (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```
This is a proper Fixpoint on T ✓.

Lemmas:
```coq
Lemma LR_lc : forall T t, LR T t -> locally_closed t.
Proof. intros T; induction T; intros t H; simpl in H; tauto. Qed.

Lemma LR_SN : forall T t, LR T t -> SN t.

Now the abstraction lemma:
```coq
Lemma LR_abs_app : forall A B body u,
  locally_closed (tm_abs A body) ->
  (forall u, LR A u -> LR B (open body u)) ->
  LR A u ->
  LR B (tm_app (tm_abs A body) u).
Proof.
  intros A B body u Hlc Hbody.
  assert (Hgen : forall u, SN u -> LR A u -> LR B (tm_app (tm_abs A body) u)).
  { intros u Hsn. induction Hsn as [u Hstepu IHu]. intros HRu.
    apply (LR_of_red_nonabs B (tm_app (tm_abs A body) u)).
    - (* lc *) split? apply lc_app...
    - (* nonabs *) discriminate.
    - (* SN *) apply SN_intro. intros t' Hs. ...
    - (* reducts *) intros t' Hs. ...
  }
  intros HRu. apply Hgen; [apply LR_SN with (T := A) | exact HRu].
Qed.
```
Note: `locally_closed (tm_abs A body)` = lc_at 0 (tm_abs A body) = lc_at 1 body. For lc of (tm_app (tm_abs A body) u): lc_app requires lc_at 0 (tm_abs A body) ✓ and lc_at 0 u ✓ (LR A u).

Reducts of `tm_app (tm_abs A body) u`:
- ST_AppAbs T' body' v Hlc' Hval: (tm_abs A body) = tm_abs T' body' → A = T', body = body'; v = u; t' = open body u → LR B(open body u) via Hbody with HRu ✓.
- ST_App1 t1 t1' t2 Hstep Hlc2: t1 = tm_abs A body and step (tm_abs A body) t1' → contradiction with value_no_step ✓ (value via v_abs with Hlc ✓).
- ST_App2 v1 t2 t2' Hval Hstep: v1 = tm_abs A body, t2 = u, t' = tm_app (tm_abs A body) t2'. Apply IHu t2' Hstep with LR A t2' from forward closure of HRu ✓.
